srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/%CE%A4%CE%B5%CF%84%CF%81%CE%B1%CE%B5%CF%85%CE%AC%CE%B3%CE%B3%CE%B5%CE%BB%CE%BF_%CE%95140_%CF%84%CE%B7%CF%82_%CE%9C%CE%B5%CE%B3%CE%AF%CF%83%CF%84%CE%B7%CF%82_%CE%9B%CE%B1%CF%8D%CF%81%CE%B1%CF%82._%CE%95%CF%85%CE%B1%CE%B3%CE%B3%CE%B5%CE%BB%CE%B9%CF%83%CF%84%CE%AE%CF%82_%CE%9B%CE%BF%CF%85%CE%BA%CE%AC%CF%82._15%CE%BF%CF%82_%CE%B1%CE%B9%CF%8E%CE%BD%CE%B1%CF%82.jpg/330px-%CE%A4%CE%B5%CF%84%CF%81%CE%B1%CE%B5%CF%85%CE%AC%CE%B3%CE%B3%CE%B5%CE%BB%CE%BF_%CE%95140_%CF%84%CE%B7%CF%82_%CE%9C%CE%B5%CE%B3%CE%AF%CF%83%CF%84%CE%B7%CF%82_%CE%9B%CE%B1%CF%8D%CF%81%CE%B1%CF%82._%CE%95%CF%85%CE%B1%CE%B3%CE%B3%CE%B5%CE%BB%CE%B9%CF%83%CF%84%CE%AE%CF%82_%CE%9B%CE%BF%CF%85%CE%BA%CE%AC%CF%82._15%CE%BF%CF%82_%CE%B1%CE%B9%CF%8E%CE%BD%CE%B1%CF%82.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a7/%CE%A4%CE%B5%CF%84%CF%81%CE%B1%CE%B5%CF%85%CE%AC%CE%B3%CE%B3%CE%B5%CE%BB%CE%BF_%CE%95140_%CF%84%CE%B7%CF%82_%CE%9C%CE%B5%CE%B3%CE%AF%CF%83%CF%84%CE%B7%CF%82_%CE%9B%CE%B1%CF%8D%CF%81%CE%B1%CF%82._%CE%95%CF%85%CE%B1%CE%B3%CE%B3%CE%B5%CE%BB%CE%B9%CF%83%CF%84%CE%AE%CF%82_%CE%9B%CE%BF%CF%85%CE%BA%CE%AC%CF%82._15%CE%BF%CF%82_%CE%B1%CE%B9%CF%8E%CE%BD%CE%B1%CF%82.jpg/440px-%CE%A4%CE%B5%CF%84%CF%81%CE%B1%CE%B5%CF%85%CE%AC%CE%B3%CE%B3%CE%B5%CE%BB%CE%BF_%CE%95140_%CF%84%CE%B7%CF%82_%CE%9C%CE%B5%CE%B3%CE%AF%CF%83%CF%84%CE%B7%CF%82_%CE%9B%CE%B1%CF%8D%CF%81%CE%B1%CF%82._%CE%95%CF%85%CE%B1%CE%B3%CE%B3%CE%B5%CE%BB%CE%B9%CF%83%CF%84%CE%AE%CF%82_%CE%9B%CE%BF%CF%85%CE%BA%CE%AC%CF%82._15%CE%BF%CF%82_%CE%B1%CE%B9%CF%8E%CE%BD%CE%B1%CF%82.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1075" data-file-height="1600" /></a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="line-height:1.2em; padding-right:0.65em;">Occupation</th><td class="infobox-data role" style="line-height:1.4em;"><a href="/wiki/Christian_missionary" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian missionary">Christian missionary</a> and <a href="/wiki/Historian" title="Historian">Historian</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="line-height:1.2em; padding-right:0.65em;">Language</th><td class="infobox-data" style="line-height:1.4em;"><a href="/wiki/Greek_language" title="Greek language">Greek</a></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label" style="line-height:1.2em; padding-right:0.65em;">Notable works</th><td class="infobox-data" style="line-height:1.4em;"><a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke" title="Gospel of Luke">Gospel of Luke</a> and <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_the_Apostles" title="Acts of the Apostles">Acts</a></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Luke the Evangelist</b> (<a href="/wiki/Latin" title="Latin">Latin</a>: <i><a href="/wiki/Lucas_(given_name)" title="Lucas (given name)">Lucas</a></i>; <a href="/wiki/Ancient_Greek_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Ancient Greek language">Ancient Greek</a>: <span lang="grc">Λουκᾶς</span>, <i><a href="/wiki/Loukas" title="Loukas">Loukâs</a></i>; <a href="/wiki/Hebrew_language" title="Hebrew language">Hebrew</a>: <span lang="he" dir="rtl">לוקאס</span>, <i>Lūqās</i>; <a href="/wiki/Imperial_Aramaic_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Imperial Aramaic language">Imperial Aramaic</a>: <span lang="arc" dir="rtl">/ܠܘܩܐ לוקא</span>, <i>Lūqā’; <a href="/wiki/Ge%CA%BDez" title="Geʽez">Ge'ez</a>: ሉቃስ</i>) is one of the <a href="/wiki/Four_Evangelists" title="Four Evangelists">Four Evangelists</a>—the four traditionally ascribed authors of the <a href="/wiki/Canonical_gospels" class="mw-redirect" title="Canonical gospels">canonical gospels</a>. The Early <a href="/wiki/Church_Fathers" title="Church Fathers">Church Fathers</a> ascribed to him authorship of both the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Luke" title="Gospel of Luke">Gospel of Luke</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Acts_of_the_Apostles" title="Acts of the Apostles">Acts of the Apostles</a>. Prominent figures in early Christianity such as <a href="/wiki/Jerome" title="Jerome">Jerome</a> and <a href="/wiki/Eusebius" title="Eusebius">Eusebius</a> later reaffirmed his authorship, although a lack of conclusive evidence as to the identity of the author of the works has led to <a href="/wiki/Authorship_of_Luke%E2%80%93Acts" title="Authorship of Luke–Acts">discussion</a> in scholarly circles, both secular and religious.
</p><p>The New Testament mentions Luke briefly a few times, and the <a href="/wiki/Epistle_to_the_Colossians" title="Epistle to the Colossians">Epistle to the Colossians</a><sup class="nourlexpansion reference"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=Col+4%3A14&amp;version=NRSV">&#91;Col 4:14&#93;</a></sup> refers to him as a physician (from Greek for 'one who heals'); thus he is thought to have been both a physician and a disciple of <a href="/wiki/Paul_the_Apostle" title="Paul the Apostle">Paul</a>.
</p><p>Since the early years of the faith, Christians have regarded him as a <a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">saint</a>. He is believed to have been a <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyr</a>, reportedly having been hanged from an olive tree, though some believe otherwise.<sup id="cite_ref-martyred?_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-martyred?-3">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> and other major denominations venerate him as <b>Saint Luke the Evangelist</b> and as a <a href="/wiki/Patron_saint" title="Patron saint">patron saint</a> of artists, physicians, <a href="/wiki/Bachelors" class="mw-redirect" title="Bachelors">bachelors</a>, surgeons, students and butchers; his <a href="/wiki/Feast_day" class="mw-redirect" title="Feast day">feast day</a> is 18 October.<sup id="cite_ref-catholicnewsagency.com_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-catholicnewsagency.com-4">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Life">Life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Luke_the_Evangelist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Evangelist_Lucas.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Evangelist_Lucas.jpg/220px-Evangelist_Lucas.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="217" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Evangelist_Lucas.jpg/330px-Evangelist_Lucas.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/6a/Evangelist_Lucas.jpg/440px-Evangelist_Lucas.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2484" data-file-height="2451" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Evangelist_Lucas.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Print of Luke the Evangelist. Made by Crispijn van de Passe de Oude.<sup id="cite_ref-ugent.be_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-ugent.be-6">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup></div></div></div>
<p>Many scholars believe that Luke was a physician who lived in the <a href="/wiki/Hellenistic_period" title="Hellenistic period">Hellenistic</a> city of <a href="/wiki/Antioch,_Turkey" class="mw-redirect" title="Antioch, Turkey">Antioch</a> in Ancient <a href="/wiki/Syria_(Roman_province)" class="mw-redirect" title="Syria (Roman province)">Syria</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-Hackett_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hackett-7">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup> although some other scholars and theologians think Luke was a <a href="/wiki/Hellenistic_Judaism" title="Hellenistic Judaism">Hellenic Jew</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHarris1980266–68_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1980266–68-8">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrelan2013102–10_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrelan2013102–10-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> While it has been widely accepted that the theology of <a href="/wiki/Luke%E2%80%93Acts" title="Luke–Acts">Luke–Acts</a> points to a <a href="/wiki/Gentile" title="Gentile">gentile</a> Christian writing for a gentile audience, some have  concluded that it is more plausible that Luke–Acts is directed to a community made up of both Jewish and gentile Christians since there is stress on the scriptural roots of the gentile mission (see the use of Isaiah 49:6 in Luke–Acts).<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKoet1989157–58_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKoet1989157–58-10">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKoet20064–5_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKoet20064–5-11">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Others have only been prepared to conclude that Luke was either a Hellenistic Jew or a <a href="/wiki/God-fearer" title="God-fearer">God-fearer</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrelan2013102–10_9-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrelan2013102–10-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> DNA testing on what <a href="/wiki/Christian_tradition" title="Christian tradition">Christian tradition</a> holds to be his body has revealed that he was of <a href="/wiki/Syrians" title="Syrians">Syrian</a> ancestry.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Whether Luke was a Jew or gentile, or something in between, it is clear from the quality of the Greek language used in Luke-Acts that he was one of the most highly educated of the authors of the New Testament.  His conscious and intentional allusions and references to, and quotations of, ancient Classical and Hellenistic Greek authors, such as Homer, Aesop, Epimenides, Euripides, Plato, and Aratus indicate that he was familiar with actual Greek literary texts.  This familiarity most likely derived from his experiences as a youth of the very homogeneous Hellenistic educational curriculum (ἐγκύκλιος παιδεία/<i>enkyklios paideia</i>) that had been, and would continue to be, used for centuries throughout the eastern Mediterranean.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Luke's earliest mention is in <a href="/wiki/Epistle_to_Philemon" title="Epistle to Philemon">Philemon</a> 1:24.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> He is also mentioned in <span class="nourlexpansion nowrap"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=Colossians+4%3A14&amp;version=NRSV">Colossians 4:14</a></span> and <span class="nourlexpansion nowrap"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=2+Timothy+4%3A11&amp;version=NRSV">2 Timothy 4:11</a></span>, two <a href="/wiki/Pauline_epistles" title="Pauline epistles">Pauline epistles</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMilligan2006149_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMilligan2006149-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMornin200674_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMornin200674-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAherne1910_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAherne1910-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmith1935792_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmith1935792-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEvon_Harnack19075_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEvon_Harnack19075-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> The next earliest account of Luke is in the <i>Anti-<a href="/wiki/Marcionite" class="mw-redirect" title="Marcionite">Marcionite</a> Prologue to the Gospel of Luke</i>, a document once thought to date to the 2nd century, but which has more recently been dated to the later 4th century.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (September 2015)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Brooklyn_Museum_-_Saint_Luke_(Saint_Luc)_-_James_Tissot.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Brooklyn_Museum_-_Saint_Luke_%28Saint_Luc%29_-_James_Tissot.jpg/170px-Brooklyn_Museum_-_Saint_Luke_%28Saint_Luc%29_-_James_Tissot.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="228" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Brooklyn_Museum_-_Saint_Luke_%28Saint_Luc%29_-_James_Tissot.jpg/255px-Brooklyn_Museum_-_Saint_Luke_%28Saint_Luc%29_-_James_Tissot.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/Brooklyn_Museum_-_Saint_Luke_%28Saint_Luc%29_-_James_Tissot.jpg/340px-Brooklyn_Museum_-_Saint_Luke_%28Saint_Luc%29_-_James_Tissot.jpg 2x" data-file-width="573" data-file-height="768" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Brooklyn_Museum_-_Saint_Luke_(Saint_Luc)_-_James_Tissot.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/James_Tissot" title="James Tissot">James Tissot</a>, <i>Saint Luke</i>, <a href="/wiki/Brooklyn_Museum" title="Brooklyn Museum">Brooklyn Museum</a></div></div></div>
<p><a href="/wiki/Epiphanius_of_Salamis" title="Epiphanius of Salamis">Epiphanius</a> states that Luke was one of the <a href="/wiki/Seventy_Apostles" class="mw-redirect" title="Seventy Apostles">Seventy Apostles</a> (<i><a href="/wiki/Panarion" title="Panarion">Panarion</a></i> 51.11), and <a href="/wiki/John_Chrysostom" title="John Chrysostom">John Chrysostom</a> indicates at one point that the "brother" that Paul mentions in the <a href="/wiki/Second_Epistle_to_the_Corinthians" title="Second Epistle to the Corinthians">Second Epistle to the Corinthians</a> 8:18 is either Luke or <a href="/wiki/Barnabas" title="Barnabas">Barnabas</a> (<i>Homily 18 on Second Corinthians</i> on 2 Corinthians 8:18).
</p><p>If one accepts that Luke was indeed the author of the Gospel bearing his name and the Acts of the Apostles, certain details of his personal life can be reasonably assumed. While he does exclude himself from those who were eyewitnesses to Jesus' ministry, he repeatedly uses the word "we" in describing the Pauline missions in Acts of the Apostles, indicating that he was personally there at those times.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBartlet1911_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBartlet1911-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Bodleian_Library_MS._Arm._d.13._Armenian_Gospels-0043-0.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Bodleian_Library_MS._Arm._d.13._Armenian_Gospels-0043-0.jpg/170px-Bodleian_Library_MS._Arm._d.13._Armenian_Gospels-0043-0.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="270" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Bodleian_Library_MS._Arm._d.13._Armenian_Gospels-0043-0.jpg/255px-Bodleian_Library_MS._Arm._d.13._Armenian_Gospels-0043-0.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Bodleian_Library_MS._Arm._d.13._Armenian_Gospels-0043-0.jpg/340px-Bodleian_Library_MS._Arm._d.13._Armenian_Gospels-0043-0.jpg 2x" data-file-width="669" data-file-height="1062" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Bodleian_Library_MS._Arm._d.13._Armenian_Gospels-0043-0.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Saint Luke as depicted in the head-piece of an Armenian Gospel manuscript from 1609, held at the <a href="/wiki/Bodleian_Library" title="Bodleian Library">Bodleian Library</a></div></div></div>
<p>The composition of the writings, as well as the range of vocabulary used, indicate that the author was an educated man. A quote in the <a href="/wiki/Epistle_to_the_Colossians" title="Epistle to the Colossians">Epistle to the Colossians</a> differentiates between Luke and other colleagues "of the <a href="/wiki/Circumcision_controversy_in_early_Christianity" title="Circumcision controversy in early Christianity">circumcision</a>."
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>¹⁰My fellow prisoner Aristarchus sends you his greetings, as does Mark, the cousin of Barnabas.  ¹¹Jesus, who is called Justus, also sends greetings. These are the only Jews among my co-workers for the kingdom of God, and they have proved a comfort to me. …¹⁴Our dear friend Luke, the doctor, and Demas send greetings.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite><span class="nourlexpansion nowrap"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=Colossians+4%3A10%E2%80%9311%2C+14&amp;version=NRSV">Colossians 4:10–11, 14</a></span>.</cite></div></blockquote>
<p>This comment has traditionally caused commentators to conclude that Luke was a gentile. If this were true, it would make Luke the only writer of the New Testament who can clearly be identified as not being Jewish. However, that is not the only possibility. Although Luke is considered likely to have been a gentile Christian, some scholars believe him to have been a <a href="/wiki/Hellenistic_Jew" class="mw-redirect" title="Hellenistic Jew">Hellenized Jew</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHarris1980266–68_8-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHarris1980266–68-8">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStrelan2013102–10_9-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStrelan2013102–10-9">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcCall1996_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcCall1996-21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup> The phrase could just as easily be used to differentiate between those <a href="/wiki/Jewish_Christians" class="mw-redirect" title="Jewish Christians">Christians who strictly observed the rituals of Judaism</a> and those who did not.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBartlet1911_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBartlet1911-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Luke's presence in <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a> with the Apostle Paul near the end of Paul's life was attested by 2 Timothy 4:11: "Only Luke is with me". In the last chapter of the Book of Acts, widely attributed to Luke, there are several accounts in the first person also affirming Luke's presence in Rome, including <span class="nourlexpansion nowrap"><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.biblegateway.com/passage/?search=Acts+28%3A16&amp;version=NRSV">Acts 28:16</a></span>: "And when we came to Rome…" According to some accounts, Luke also contributed to the <a href="/wiki/Authorship_of_the_Epistle_to_the_Hebrews" title="Authorship of the Epistle to the Hebrews">authorship of the Epistle to the Hebrews</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFonck1910_22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFonck1910-22">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Luke died at age 84 in <a href="/wiki/Boeotia" title="Boeotia">Boeotia</a>, according to a "fairly early and widespread tradition".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEButler1991342_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEButler1991342-23">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> According to <a href="/wiki/Nikephoros_Kallistos_Xanthopoulos" title="Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos">Nikephoros Kallistos Xanthopoulos</a>, Greek historian of the 14th century (and others), Luke's tomb was located in <a href="/wiki/Thebes_(Greece)" class="mw-redirect" title="Thebes (Greece)">Thebes</a>, whence his relics were transferred to <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a> in the year 357.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMigne1901cols_875–78_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMigne1901cols_875–78-24">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Authorship_of_Luke_and_Acts">Authorship of Luke and Acts</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Luke_the_Evangelist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Authorship of Luke and Acts">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Authorship_of_Luke%E2%80%93Acts" title="Authorship of Luke–Acts">Authorship of Luke–Acts</a></div>
<p>The Gospel of Luke does not name its author.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESanders199563–64_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESanders199563–64-25">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman200043_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman200043-26">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESeniorAchtemeierKarris2002328_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESeniorAchtemeierKarris2002328-27">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENickle200143_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENickle200143-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> The Gospel was not, nor does it claim to be, written by direct witnesses to the reported events, unlike Acts beginning in the sixteenth chapter.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2005235_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2005235-29">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2004110_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2004110-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006143_31-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2006143-31">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup> However, in most translations the author suggests that they have investigated the book's events and notes the name (Theophilus) of that to whom they are writing.
</p><p>The earliest manuscript of the Gospel (Papyrus 75 = Papyrus Bodmer XIV-XV), dated <span title="circa">c.</span> AD&#160;200, ascribes the work to Luke; as did <a href="/wiki/Irenaeus" title="Irenaeus">Irenaeus</a> writing <span title="circa">c.</span> AD&#160;180, and the <a href="/wiki/Muratorian_fragment" title="Muratorian fragment">Muratorian fragment</a>, a 7th-century Latin manuscript thought to be copied and translated from a Greek manuscript as old as AD&#160;170.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrown1997267_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown1997267-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The Gospel of Luke and the Acts of the Apostles make up a two-volume work which scholars call <a href="/wiki/Luke%E2%80%93Acts" title="Luke–Acts">Luke–Acts</a>. Together they account for 27.5% of the <a href="/wiki/New_Testament" title="New Testament">New Testament</a>, the largest contribution by a single author.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoring2012556_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoring2012556-33">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:De_heilige_Lucas_schildert_de_Madonna_-_Maarten_van_Heemskerck-1532.JPG" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/De_heilige_Lucas_schildert_de_Madonna_-_Maarten_van_Heemskerck-1532.JPG/220px-De_heilige_Lucas_schildert_de_Madonna_-_Maarten_van_Heemskerck-1532.JPG" decoding="async" width="220" height="160" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/De_heilige_Lucas_schildert_de_Madonna_-_Maarten_van_Heemskerck-1532.JPG/330px-De_heilige_Lucas_schildert_de_Madonna_-_Maarten_van_Heemskerck-1532.JPG 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/16/De_heilige_Lucas_schildert_de_Madonna_-_Maarten_van_Heemskerck-1532.JPG/440px-De_heilige_Lucas_schildert_de_Madonna_-_Maarten_van_Heemskerck-1532.JPG 2x" data-file-width="800" data-file-height="580" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:De_heilige_Lucas_schildert_de_Madonna_-_Maarten_van_Heemskerck-1532.JPG" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i>St. Luke painting the Virgin</i>, by <a href="/wiki/Maarten_van_Heemskerck" title="Maarten van Heemskerck">Maarten van Heemskerck</a>, 1532</div></div></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="As_a_historian">As a historian</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Luke_the_Evangelist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: As a historian">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">See also: <a href="/wiki/Historical_reliability_of_the_Acts_of_the_Apostles" title="Historical reliability of the Acts of the Apostles">Historical reliability of the Acts of the Apostles</a>, <a href="/wiki/Census_of_Quirinius" title="Census of Quirinius">Census of Quirinius</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Chronology_of_Jesus" title="Chronology of Jesus">Chronology of Jesus</a></div>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Luke_by_roslin.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Luke_by_roslin.jpg/170px-Luke_by_roslin.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="248" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/00/Luke_by_roslin.jpg/255px-Luke_by_roslin.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/0/00/Luke_by_roslin.jpg 2x" data-file-width="274" data-file-height="400" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Luke_by_roslin.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Saint Luke the Evangelist, a medieval Armenian illumination by <a href="/wiki/Toros_Roslin" title="Toros Roslin">Toros Roslin</a></div></div></div>
<p>Most scholars understand Luke's works (<a href="/wiki/Luke%E2%80%93Acts" title="Luke–Acts">Luke–Acts</a>) in the tradition of <a href="/wiki/Greek_historiography" class="mw-redirect" title="Greek historiography">Greek historiography</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrant1963Ch._10_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrant1963Ch._10-34">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> Luke 1:1–4, drawing on historical investigation, identified the work to the readers as belonging to the genre of history.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBauckham2017117_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBauckham2017117-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup> There is disagreement about how best to treat Luke's writings, with some historians regarding Luke as highly accurate,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamsay1915222_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamsay1915222-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlaiklock197096_37-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlaiklock197096-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> and others taking a more critical approach.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPowell19896_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPowell19896-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcGrew2019_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGrew2019-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFlew1966_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlew1966-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBradley187444_41-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBradley187444-41">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-McGrew&#39;s_conclusion_42-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-McGrew&#39;s_conclusion-42">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Based on his accurate description of towns, cities and islands, as well as correctly naming various official titles, archaeologist <a href="/wiki/William_Mitchell_Ramsay" title="William Mitchell Ramsay">William Mitchell Ramsay</a> wrote that "Luke is a historian of the first rank; not merely are his statements of fact trustworthy.  …[He] should be placed along with the very greatest of historians."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTERamsay1915222_36-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTERamsay1915222-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup> Professor of Classics at <a href="/wiki/Auckland_University" class="mw-redirect" title="Auckland University">Auckland University</a>, <a href="/wiki/Edward_Musgrave_Blaiklock" title="Edward Musgrave Blaiklock">Edward Musgrave Blaiklock</a>, wrote: "For accuracy of detail, and for evocation of atmosphere, Luke stands, in fact, with <a href="/wiki/Thucydides" title="Thucydides">Thucydides</a>. The Acts of the Apostles is not shoddy product of pious imagining, but a trustworthy record.  …It was the spadework of archaeology which first revealed the truth."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlaiklock197096_37-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlaiklock197096-37">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup> New Testament scholar Colin Hemer has made a number of advancements in understanding the historical nature and accuracy of Luke's writings.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHemer1989104–7_43-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHemer1989104–7-43">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>On the purpose of Acts, New Testament scholar <a href="/wiki/Luke_Timothy_Johnson" title="Luke Timothy Johnson">Luke Timothy Johnson</a> has noted that "Luke's account is selected and shaped to suit his apologetic interests, not in defiance of but in conformity to ancient standards of historiography."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJohnson1991474_44-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJohnson1991474-44">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> Such a position is shared by Richard Heard, who sees historical deficiencies as arising from "special objects in writing and to the limitations of his sources of information."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHeard1950Ch._13:_The_Acts_of_the_Apostles_45-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHeard1950Ch._13:_The_Acts_of_the_Apostles-45">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In modern times, Luke's competence as a historian is questioned, depending upon one's <i>a priori</i> view of the <a href="/wiki/Supernatural" title="Supernatural">supernatural</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPowell19896_38-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPowell19896-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> Since post-<a href="/wiki/Age_of_Enlightenment" title="Age of Enlightenment">Enlightenment</a> historians work with <a href="/wiki/Methodological_naturalism" class="mw-redirect" title="Methodological naturalism">methodological naturalism</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEhrman2000229_46-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEhrman2000229-46">&#91;43&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMcGrew2019_39-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMcGrew2019-39">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFlew1966_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFlew1966-40">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBradley187444_41-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBradley187444-41">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-McGrew&#39;s_conclusion_42-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-McGrew&#39;s_conclusion-42">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-CraigEhrman2006_47-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-CraigEhrman2006-47">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup> such historians would see a narrative that relates supernatural, fantastic things like angels, demons etc., as problematic as a historical source. <a href="/wiki/Mark_Allan_Powell" title="Mark Allan Powell">Mark Powell</a> claims that "it is doubtful whether the writing of history was ever Luke's intent. Luke wrote to proclaim, to persuade, and to interpret; he did not write to preserve records for posterity. An awareness of this, has been, for many, the final nail in Luke the historian's coffin."<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPowell19896_38-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPowell19896-38">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Robert_M._Grant_(theologian)" title="Robert M. Grant (theologian)">Robert M. Grant</a> has noted that although Luke saw himself within the historical tradition, his work contains a number of statistical improbabilities, such as the sizable crowd addressed by <a href="/wiki/Saint_Peter" title="Saint Peter">Peter</a> in Acts 4:4. He has also noted chronological difficulties whereby Luke "has <a href="/wiki/Gamaliel" title="Gamaliel">Gamaliel</a> refer to <a href="/wiki/Theudas" title="Theudas">Theudas</a> and <a href="/wiki/Judas_of_Galilee" title="Judas of Galilee">Judas</a> in the wrong order, and Theudas actually rebelled about a decade after Gamaliel spoke (5:36–7)",<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrant1963Ch._10_34-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrant1963Ch._10-34">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> though this report's status as a chronological difficulty is hotly disputed.<sup id="cite_ref-48" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-48">&#91;44&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-49" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-49">&#91;45&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Brent Landau writes:
</p>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>So how do we account for a Gospel that is believable about minor events but implausible about a major one? One possible explanation is that Luke believed that Jesus’ birth was of such importance for the entire world that he dramatically juxtaposed this event against an (imagined) act of worldwide domination by a Roman emperor who was himself called “savior” and “son of God”—but who was nothing of the sort. For an ancient historian following in the footsteps of Thucydides, such a procedure would have been perfectly acceptable.<sup id="cite_ref-Landau_50-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Landau-50">&#91;46&#93;</a></sup></p></blockquote>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="As_an_artist">As an artist</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Luke_the_Evangelist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: As an artist">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Evangelist_Luka_pishustchiy_ikonu.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Evangelist_Luka_pishustchiy_ikonu.jpg/170px-Evangelist_Luka_pishustchiy_ikonu.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="233" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Evangelist_Luka_pishustchiy_ikonu.jpg/255px-Evangelist_Luka_pishustchiy_ikonu.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/2/21/Evangelist_Luka_pishustchiy_ikonu.jpg 2x" data-file-width="329" data-file-height="450" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Evangelist_Luka_pishustchiy_ikonu.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Luke the Evangelist painting the first <a href="/wiki/Icon" title="Icon">icon</a> of the Virgin Mary</div></div></div>
<p>Christian tradition, starting from the 8th century, states that Luke was the first <a href="/wiki/Icon" title="Icon">icon</a> painter. He is said to have painted pictures of the <a href="/wiki/Mary_(mother_of_Jesus)" class="mw-redirect" title="Mary (mother of Jesus)">Virgin Mary</a> and Child, in particular the <a href="/wiki/Hodegetria" title="Hodegetria">Hodegetria</a> image in <a href="/wiki/Constantinople" title="Constantinople">Constantinople</a> (now lost). Starting from the 11th century, a number of painted images were venerated as his autograph works, including the <a href="/wiki/Black_Madonna_of_Cz%C4%99stochowa" title="Black Madonna of Częstochowa">Black Madonna of Częstochowa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Theotokos_of_Vladimir" class="mw-redirect" title="Theotokos of Vladimir">Our Lady of Vladimir</a>, and <i><a href="/wiki/Maria_Advocata_(Madonna_del_Rosario)" title="Maria Advocata (Madonna del Rosario)">Madonna del Rosario</a></i>. He was also said to have painted Saints <a href="/wiki/Saint_Peter" title="Saint Peter">Peter</a> and Paul, and to have illustrated a gospel book with a full cycle of miniatures.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEGrigg19873–9_51-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEGrigg19873–9-51">&#91;47&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Bacci_52-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Bacci-52">&#91;e&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Late medieval <a href="/wiki/Guild_of_Saint_Luke" title="Guild of Saint Luke">Guilds of Saint Luke</a> in the cities of Late Medieval Europe, especially <a href="/wiki/Flanders" title="Flanders">Flanders</a>, or the "<a href="/wiki/Accademia_di_San_Luca" title="Accademia di San Luca">Accademia di San Luca</a>" (Academy of Saint Luke) in Rome—imitated in many other European cities during the 16th century—gathered together and protected painters. The tradition that Luke painted icons of Mary and Jesus has been common, particularly in <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodoxy" title="Eastern Orthodoxy">Eastern Orthodoxy</a>. The tradition also has support from the <a href="/wiki/Saint_Thomas_Christians" title="Saint Thomas Christians">Saint Thomas Christians</a> of India who claim to still have one of the <a href="/wiki/Theotokos" title="Theotokos">Theotokos</a> icons that Saint Luke painted and which <a href="/wiki/Thomas_the_Apostle" title="Thomas the Apostle">Saint Thomas</a> brought to India.<sup id="cite_ref-Hosten_53-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Hosten-53">&#91;f&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The art critic <a href="https://ru.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D0%A3%D1%81%D0%BF%D0%B5%D0%BD%D1%81%D0%BA%D0%B8%D0%B9,_%D0%90%D0%BB%D0%B5%D0%BA%D1%81%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%B4%D1%80_%D0%98%D0%B2%D0%B0%D0%BD%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%B8%D1%87_(%D0%B8%D1%81%D0%BA%D1%83%D1%81%D1%81%D1%82%D0%B2%D0%BE%D0%B2%D0%B5%D0%B4)" class="extiw" title="ru:Успенский, Александр Иванович (искусствовед)">A. I. Uspensky</a> writes that the icons attributed to the brush of the Evangelist Luke have a completely Byzantine character that was fully established only in the 5th-6th centuries.<sup id="cite_ref-54" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-54">&#91;48&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Symbol">Symbol</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Luke_the_Evangelist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Symbol">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Hermen_Rode_001.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Hermen_Rode_001.jpg/170px-Hermen_Rode_001.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="289" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Hermen_Rode_001.jpg/255px-Hermen_Rode_001.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/64/Hermen_Rode_001.jpg/340px-Hermen_Rode_001.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1256" data-file-height="2135" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Hermen_Rode_001.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Winged altar of the Guild of Saint Luke, <a href="/wiki/Hermen_Rode" title="Hermen Rode">Hermen Rode</a>, <a href="/wiki/L%C3%BCbeck" title="Lübeck">Lübeck</a> (1484)</div></div></div>
<p>In traditional depictions, such as paintings, <a href="/wiki/Evangelist_portrait" title="Evangelist portrait">evangelist portraits</a>, and church <a href="/wiki/Mosaic" title="Mosaic">mosaics</a>, Saint Luke is often accompanied by an <a href="/wiki/Ox" title="Ox">ox</a> or <a href="/wiki/Bull" title="Bull">bull</a>, usually having wings. Sometimes only the symbol is shown, especially when in a combination of those of all <a href="/wiki/Four_Evangelists" title="Four Evangelists">Four Evangelists</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEZuffi20038_55-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEZuffi20038-55">&#91;49&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAudsleyAudsley186594_56-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAudsleyAudsley186594-56">&#91;50&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Veneration">Veneration</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Luke_the_Evangelist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Veneration">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>

Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.</span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">October 2022</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a> <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">commemorated</a> Saint Luke,<sup id="cite_ref-57" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-57">&#91;51&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Seventy_disciples" title="Seventy disciples">Apostle of the Seventy</a>, <a href="/wiki/Four_Evangelists" title="Four Evangelists">Evangelist</a>, companion (coworker) of the <a href="/wiki/Apostle_Paul" class="mw-redirect" title="Apostle Paul">holy Apostle Paul</a>, hieromartyr, physician, first icon painter on:
</p>
<ul><li>22 April – Feast of Apostles of the Seventy: <a href="/wiki/Nathanael_(follower_of_Jesus)" title="Nathanael (follower of Jesus)">Nathaniel (Nathanael)</a>, Luke the Evangelist, <a href="/wiki/Clement_of_Sardice" title="Clement of Sardice">Clement of Sardice</a> or <a href="/wiki/Clement_of_Rome" title="Clement of Rome">Clement of Rome</a> and <a href="/wiki/Apelles_of_Heraklion" title="Apelles of Heraklion">Apelles of Heraklion</a> (Greek sources say that Saint Luke (Loukias) was someone other than the Evangelist Luke), the commoration is held second time commemorated together on 10 September.</li>
<li>20 June – Translation of the relics and garments of the Apostles Luke, <a href="/wiki/Andrew_the_Apostle" title="Andrew the Apostle">Andrew</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Thomas_the_Apostle" title="Thomas the Apostle">Thomas</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Prophet_Eliseus" class="mw-redirect" title="Prophet Eliseus">Prophet Eliseus</a>, and Martyr Lazarus of Persia found <span title="circa">c.</span><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;960</span>, during the time of the emperor <a href="/wiki/Romanos_I_Lekapenos" title="Romanos I Lekapenos">Romanos Lakapenos</a> (919–44) in a monastery of Saint Augusta into the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_the_Holy_Apostles" title="Church of the Holy Apostles">Church of the Holy Apostles in Constantinople</a> under <a href="/wiki/Constantine_VII" title="Constantine VII">Emperor Constantine Porphyrogenitus</a> (c. 956–70) by <a href="/wiki/Polyeuctus_of_Constantinople" title="Polyeuctus of Constantinople">Saint Patriarch Polyeuctus of Constantinople</a> (956–70).</li>
<li>10 September – Feast of Apostles of the Seventy: <a href="/wiki/Nathanael_(follower_of_Jesus)" title="Nathanael (follower of Jesus)">Nathaniel (Nathanael)</a>, Luke the Evangelist, <a href="/wiki/Clement_of_Sardice" title="Clement of Sardice">Clement of Sardice</a> or <a href="/wiki/Clement_of_Rome" title="Clement of Rome">Clement of Rome</a> and <a href="/wiki/Apelles_of_Heraklion" title="Apelles of Heraklion">Apelles of Heraklion</a> (Greek sources say that Saint Luke (Loukias) was someone other than the Evangelist Luke), the commemoration is held first time commemorated together on 22 April.</li>
<li>18 October – Feast of Apostle Luke</li>
<li>4 January – Synaxis of the Seventy Apostles</li>
<li>Synaxis of All Saints of Achaia – moveable holiday on Sunday between 23 and 29 November</li>
<li>Synaxis of All Saints of Boeotia – moveable holiday on Saturday between 25 and 31 May</li></ul>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Relics">Relics</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Luke_the_Evangelist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Relics">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Eight bodies and nine heads, located in different places, are presented as the relics of the Apostle Luke.<sup id="cite_ref-58" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-58">&#91;52&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-59" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-59">&#91;53&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despot <a href="/wiki/%C4%90ura%C4%91_Brankovi%C4%87" title="Đurađ Branković">George of Serbia</a> purportedly bought the relics from the Ottoman sultan <a href="/wiki/Murad_II" title="Murad II">Murad II</a> for 30,000 gold coins. After the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_conquest_of_Bosnia" class="mw-redirect" title="Ottoman conquest of Bosnia">Ottoman conquest of Bosnia</a>, the kingdom's last queen, George's granddaughter <a href="/wiki/Mary_of_Serbia,_Queen_of_Bosnia" class="mw-redirect" title="Mary of Serbia, Queen of Bosnia">Mary</a>, who had brought the relics with her from Serbia as her dowry, sold them to the <a href="/wiki/Venetian_Republic" class="mw-redirect" title="Venetian Republic">Venetian Republic</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFine1975331_60-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFine1975331-60">&#91;54&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1992, the then <a href="/wiki/Greek_Orthodox" class="mw-redirect" title="Greek Orthodox">Greek Orthodox</a> Metropolitan <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_Ieronymos_II_of_Athens" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop Ieronymos II of Athens">Ieronymos</a> of <a href="/wiki/Thebes,_Greece" title="Thebes, Greece">Thebes and Levathia</a> (who subsequently became Archbishop Ieronymos II of <a href="/wiki/Athens" title="Athens">Athens</a> and All Greece) requested from Bishop <a href="/wiki/Antonio_Mattiazzo" title="Antonio Mattiazzo">Antonio Mattiazzo</a> of Padua the return of "a significant fragment of the relics of St. Luke to be placed on the site where the holy tomb of the Evangelist is located and venerated today". This prompted a scientific investigation of the relics in <a href="/wiki/Padua" title="Padua">Padua</a>, and by numerous lines of empirical evidence (archeological analyses of the Tomb in <a href="/wiki/Thebes,_Greece" title="Thebes, Greece">Thebes</a> and the Reliquary of <a href="/wiki/Padua" title="Padua">Padua</a>, anatomical analyses of the remains, <a href="/wiki/Carbon-14_dating" class="mw-redirect" title="Carbon-14 dating">carbon-14 dating</a>, comparison with the purported skull of the Evangelist located in <a href="/wiki/Prague" title="Prague">Prague</a>) confirmed that these were the remains of an individual of Syrian descent who died between AD 72 and AD 416.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMarinTrolese2003_61-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMarinTrolese2003-61">&#91;55&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECraig2001_62-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECraig2001-62">&#91;56&#93;</a></sup> The <a href="/wiki/Diocese_of_Padua" class="mw-redirect" title="Diocese of Padua">Bishop of Padua</a> then delivered to Metropolitan Ieronymos the rib of Saint Luke that was closest to his heart to be kept at his tomb in Thebes.<sup id="cite_ref-Tornielli_63-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Tornielli-63">&#91;57&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWade2001_64-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWade2001-64">&#91;58&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Thus, the relics of Saint Luke are divided as follows:
</p>
<ul><li>The body, in the <a href="/wiki/Abbey_of_Santa_Giustina" title="Abbey of Santa Giustina">Abbey of Santa Giustina</a> in Padua;</li>
<li>The head, in the <a href="/wiki/St._Vitus_Cathedral" title="St. Vitus Cathedral">St. Vitus Cathedral</a> in Prague;</li>
<li>A rib, at his tomb in Thebes.</li></ul>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r996844942"/><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>We also collected and typed modern samples from Syria and Greece. By comparison with these population samples, and with samples from Anatolia that were already available in the literature, we could reject the hypothesis that the body belonged to a Greek, rather than a Syrian, individual. However, the probability of an origin in the area of modern Turkey was only insignificantly lower than the probability of a Syrian origin. The genetic evidence is therefore compatible with the possibility that the body comes from Syria, but also with its replacement in Constantinople.<sup id="cite_ref-Vernesi_Benedetto_Caramelli_Secchieri_2001_p._65-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Vernesi_Benedetto_Caramelli_Secchieri_2001_p.-65">&#91;59&#93;</a></sup></p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite>Genetic characterization of the body attributed to the evangelist Luke</cite></div></blockquote>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Luke_the_Evangelist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-martyred?-3"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-martyred?_3-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFAherne1910">Aherne 1910</a> notes that it is controversial whether he actually died a martyr's death</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Hackett-7"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Hackett_7-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Luke, was born in <a href="/wiki/Antioch" title="Antioch">Antioch</a>, by profession was a physician.<a href="#CITEREFHackett1858">Hackett 1858</a>, p.&#160;12 He had become a disciple of the apostle Paul and later followed Paul until his [Paul's] martyrdom. He died at the age of 84 years.<a href="#CITEREFHackett1858">Hackett 1858</a>, p.&#160;335 </span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-McGrew&#39;s_conclusion-42"><span class="mw-cite-backlink">^ <a href="#cite_ref-McGrew&#39;s_conclusion_42-0"><sup><i><b>a</b></i></sup></a> <a href="#cite_ref-McGrew&#39;s_conclusion_42-1"><sup><i><b>b</b></i></sup></a></span> <span class="reference-text">McGrew's conclusion: historians work with <a href="/wiki/Methodological_naturalism" class="mw-redirect" title="Methodological naturalism">methodological naturalism</a>, which precludes them from establishing miracles as objective historical facts;<a href="#CITEREFFlew1966">Flew 1966</a>, p.&#160;146 cf. <a href="#CITEREFBradley1874">Bradley 1874</a>, p.&#160;44.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-CraigEhrman2006-47"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-CraigEhrman2006_47-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Historians can only establish what probably happened in the past, and by definition a miracle is the least probable occurrence. And so, by the very nature of the canons of historical research, we can't claim historically that a miracle probably happened. By definition, it probably didn't. And history can only establish what probably did.<a href="#CITEREFCraigEhrman2006">Craig &amp; Ehrman 2006</a> </span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Bacci-52"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Bacci_52-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">The basic study on the legends concerning Saint Luke as a painter is <a href="#CITEREFBacci1998">Bacci 1998</a> </span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-Hosten-53"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-Hosten_53-0">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"> Father <b>H. Hosten</b> in his book <i>Antiquities</i> notes the following "The picture at the mount is one of the oldest, and, therefore, one of the most venerable Christian paintings to be had in India. Other traditions hold that St. Luke painted two icons which currently are in Greece: the "Theotokos Mega Spileotissa" (Our Lady of the Great Cave, where supposedly Saint Luke lived for a period of time in asceticism) and the "Panagia Soumela", and "Panagia Kykkou" which are in Cyprus."</span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Citations">Citations</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Luke_the_Evangelist&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Citations">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
